17 Added Annotations (Notes)   
Note 1: The specific ratios do not matter. You should not be curve fitting/overfitting your system (trying to find the best ratio). To elaborate, the logic in the example behind using 3–4 RRR in continuation trades is that you should allow for larger movements against your entry because you are entering in the middle of a trend. For example, when trend following, if you are buying, you are executing at premium prices, not at discount prices. More space for error is required. And 4–5 RRR, for example, is encouraging tighter stop losses relative to the target for reversals because you are actively going against the short-term trend. The ratios given were example ratios; you can change them based on your ideas.   
Note 2: We aim for our “Implied Backtesting Costs” to be higher than the “Realised Costs” from real time trading. We assume the costs paid are always at the extreme for strategy robustness and reduced sensitivity to collapses in real time. It is one of many ways we put strategies under stress when testing.   
Note 3: When I say ’consult logic’, I am referring to plausible order flow dynam.ics in price. which I mention in the document primarily, but it is also about rejecting ideas like ”MA bounces” and ”Fibonacci”, which are not logical reasons to engage with the markets.

Definitions   
1\.   
Constraints What limits you \-time, capital, lifestyle. These set the boundaries for what you can actually trade. Your system must respect them.

2\.   
Market Type Behaviour of a market: mean reverting, trending, or random/alternating.

3\.   
Valid Trading Window The hours when you’re allowed to trade. Based on where volume and volatility are.

4\.   
Risk (R) The set amount of capital you’re willing to lose per trade. Fixed, consistent. Example: 1R \= 3%.

5\.   
RRR Risk-to-reward ratio (e.g., 1:3 \= risk \$100 to make \$300).

6\.   
Price Mechanics Price moves because buyers and sellers are imbalanced. That’s it. It explains rejections and moves \-it’s not an edge, it’s just reality.

7\.   
3-Wick Setup Three wicks rejecting a level \-signals price has repeated selling activity and won’t break through. Must be rule-based without room for interpretation.

8\.   
Tick The smallest price increment on an instrument.

9\.   
Execution Cost Spreads, commissions, and slippage affecting net performance. Ig.nore it and your edge vanishes.

10\.   
Backtest Testing your rules on past data. Done honestly, no scrolling, no cherry-picking, no hindsight. Bar Replay below in 13\.

11\.   
Overfitting When your strategy works only on the past because you’ve shaped it to work on past historical data instead of applying and idea to historical data. Looks good in testing, fails live.

12\.   
Stress Test Deliberately run your system in bad conditions. These are notable periods of intraday chop, low volume on trend trading strategies and periods of relentless trends on mean reversion/reversal strategies. If it collapses, it’s weak. Example: Someone could be running a mean reversion day trading system on YM and he could stress test August 8th to September 13th 2024 as an example, where,

here Dow Jones exhibited strong trending behaviour which is against the system’s nature.

13\.   
Bar Replay Play charts forward candle by candle to mimic real-time. Helps you test if you’d actually take your setups live. E.g., TradingView Bar Replay

14\.   
Scaling In Adding size after entry. Must be planned and tested \-not just done because “it looks good”.

15\.   
Hedge Open a position benefiting from movements in the opposite direction. Useful at times, but messy if you don’t have clear rules.

16\.   
Breakeven/Partials Closing part/all of the trade early. Often reduces long-term edge unless justified by data.

17\.   
Ghost Liquidity Orders that aren’t visible but sit around visible levels. Cause sharp reactions or none at all. It’s just a surge of liquidity that isn’t visible on the books.

18\.   
Random Walk Price sometimes moves like noise. Most patterns don’t work unless they’re backed by logic. A Random Walk is a market that is 100% random. In other words, it is effectively a completely efficient market where no edge is possible. Real markets are of course different.

19\.   
Bracketed Limit Orders Pre-set entry, stop, and take-profit. Forces discipline. Removes intuition in real time.

20\.   
Institutional Narrative Fallacy The idea that “smart money” always leaves clues. Usually marketing fluff. If it’s not testable, it’s not valid.

21\.   
Data Snooping Repeatedly looking at a data series from different angles to confirm something that you haven’t defined ahead of time often leading to insignificant and/or biased discoveries. Essentially looking too hard for patterns and finding things that don’t actually repeat. Typically kills forward performance.

22\.   
Drawdown How far your strategy drops from peaks in tests. Crucial for knowing how big your positions should be in advance. For example, a trader could have a max losing streak of 8 but your peak to trough could be 12x your risk (some wins followed by strings of losses repeatedly create this) — Super important to track and know. That’s the maximum drawdown you should be taking into account, especially if working with prop firms.

23\.   
Dynamic Targeting Set targets based on real market structure \-swing highs, lows, clusters of wicks. not arbitrary price movements e.g., 100 points, 100 handles, 100 pips, 100 ticks. Market is too dynamic for a one size fits all.

24\.   
Expectancy The average gain or loss per trade. Strategies don’t need high win rates \-it needs consistency in the data and logical backing: Expectancy \= average win . win rate . (1 . win rate) . average loss.

25\.   
Logic-Driven Rule A rule built on how the market behaves \-not what a shape on a chart looks like or some untested theory. For example purposes only, using the 3 wicks example. Bar 1 closes with a wick high; this shows that there was selling pressure. If the next candle interacts with bar 1’s high but fails to close above, creating another wick, it shows continued selling pressure. If on bar 3 it happens again, it shows compounded selling pressure. If it reverses, it should do so quickly. If price continues beyond the wicks, price should continue trending. Using a small stop loss relative to the target can create an edge if costs are managed properly.

1 Introduction   
This is a short, focused document outlining the core principles behind building robust, high-integrity trading systems. When applied, these principles can help move traders away from fragile, overfitted approaches and towards systems that compound reliably over time.   
This knowledge is required before progressing to Volume 2 of the Strategy Engineer.ing series, ensuring that the foundations needed for robust development are properly understood.

Data collection basics   
While advanced data processing is optimal, collecting data in a simple format establishes a required baseline that exceeds standard retail practices, such as (3R \+3R 1R \+3R 1R 1R)=6R, over more than 150 trades and using it to calculate the maximum peak-to-trough and win rate represents a good starting point and exceeds what most retail traders undertake.   
A lot of traders backtest and then wonder why their “profitable” strategies fall apart in live trading. If you apply these three principles, forward tests, and live deployment should improve dramatically.

Here are some key actions to consider.   
1\. Timeframe separation before combination   
Timeframe mixing e.g., 5m and 15m. If both timeframes are used for trading entries with the same setup, you should isolate their results before combining them to measure the true effectiveness of each and prevent overlap. If you choose to run both strategies, do not prioritise one setup, as uneven priorities will make it random whether the 5m or 15m setup is executed in real time, which is noisy rather than logical (sometimes the 5m trade will randomly cancel out the 15m trade, and vice versa). Run them both without cross-timeframe interference, or use a single timeframe (e.g., only 15m).   
2\. Longs versus Shorts Analysis (directional separation)   
Why the strategy performs exceptionally on the long side versus the short side, or vice versa, must be logically justified; otherwise, this analysis falls into overfitting territory. Occasionally, a strategy will only work well for longing and vice versa and mechanical reasons can exist for it.   
In terms of longs vs shorts, either side should only be isolated in real time if there is a logic-based reason that can justify the idea that is not drawn from the data itself. For example, a long-only S\&P 500 swing approach can be justified by the positive drift caused by Quantitative Easing and consistent investment market buy flow, which creates this undercurrent.   
On the short side, the strategy’s formation depends on a fast follow-through to target, which is common in “bearish” price action (more volatile) and produces superior results. Research regarding the nature of modern market volatility supports this observation. Strategies whose logic aligns well with this can benefit.   
3\. Discarding inefficient strategies   
If I do not get an in-sample expectancy / expected value reading beyond 0.2R, I stop testing, as I would believe the strategy is underfitted or something else has gone wrong. If a small, logic-based optimisation that complements the system’s logic cannot save it, I test on a few other markets that may be compatible. If that does not work, I toss the strategy away. If the strategy does not retain at least 20% of the in-sample return on out-of-sample tests, I also discard it, as this would reveal fragility such as poor logical integrity, overdependence on a specific market condition, or overfitting.   
Definition: In sample \[IS\] is the main testing data for your strategy e.g., last 12 months. Out-of-sample \[OOS\] are tests done outside of the strategy’s main data. Underfitted is when a strategy is too simple to provide any edge. Imagine a line that is supposed to be the “best fit” but is too far away from the dots.   
Key:   
An expected value (EV) of 0.2 is equivalent to achieving any of the following:   
1\.   
A 60% win rate with a 1:1 risk-reward ratio (RRR).

2\.   
A 40% win rate with a 1:2 risk-reward ratio.

3\.   
A 30% win rate with a 1:3 risk-reward ratio.

It shows that a minor edge exists which I require before moving on to the optimisation stage.   
If you try to save something that does not work well already, you are bound to overfit; avoid falling into that trap. It is an almost guaranteed path to failure.   
Make sure your ideas are predefined.   
This is important for strategy integrity, as it helps ensure your results are not overly influenced by look-ahead bias or data snooping. Try to avoid tweaking the strategy as you go along.   
4\. Indicators Without a Mechanism Are Worthless (The Why Before What Principle) Indicators are fine when used to filter a trading system’s actions, but most have nothing of substance backing up on why they should reliably forecast a price movement besides the data it was tested on that which happened to return a positive result.   
“This indicator works in these tests” is circular reasoning and “these settings are effective” is overfitting.   
Both of these markers actively work against your chances of creating a persistent market edge, especially the latter, which degrades it significantly.   
You cannot play around to “see what works” (selection bias), you must design your strategies in advance with parameters that align logically first, only after that you validate with real market data and statistics.   
For example, if you are going to use an indicator, the settings must be adjusted to accurately fit what you are actually trying to filter, e.g., the last hour, the range of your setup’s formation (a dynamic value), the last day, or the entire session. Before any testing data is collected, the logical values to test must be set in advance to avoid destroying your strategy’s integrity.   
5\. The Why Before What Principle:   
Work out why a market behaviour should happen and why it doesn’t always happen (important); this must be grounded in a legitimate mechanism or research before you test what the data shows. Always.   
The reversed order (spot a pattern, then test it) invites statistical biases which degrade the quality of your data significantly, because you went looking for something that already looked good based on price movements which are largely random, and that randomness will not repeat 1:1 in the future.   
Modern edges are small and are magnified by repetition and leverage.   
The correct order states a falsifiable claim first, then checks the data against it.   
If the only justification is that “the backtest said it works”, the idea gets discarded. This is what most traders are doing, and this is exactly why most people’s good in-sample backtests fail in live conditions and in out-of-sample tests.   
Why before what, always. The effect:   
When you align yourself with these principles, the quantity of profitable backtests you produce will drop, but the quality of your tests will surge.

Tests with low integrity are beneath consideration.   
The effect:   
When you align yourself with these principles, the quantity of profitable backtests you produce will drop, but the quality of your tests will surge.   
Tests with low integrity are beneath consideration.   
Heads up:   
The next five pages will go into trading costs. It may feel a bit intense, but stick with it. This section aims to add the clarity and edge you require to build a robust strategy. Straight after, we revisit our strategy design principles.

3 Manual: TradingView Bar Replay   
TradingView is one of many platforms that can be used for backtesting. If your strategy is mechanical and you are used to marking the specific setup, you may not need bar replay, although I recommend it especially for beginners. Regardless, you must double-check your data by looking over it for mistakes e.g., missing losses. Do not forget to correct the future positions that are affected because of the errors you correct.   
3.1 Paying for good resources and data is almost always worth it.   
It is dozens to hundreds of USD at risk with the potential of thousands in gains. We suggest starting a trial or purchasing a subscription when you have a set of sound ideas to test if hesitant. Do not feel pressured. If you do not have ideas to test yet, you can revisit this document later. We suggest reading at a minimum all of STS’ Strategy Engineering and Microstructure material before engaging in the backtesting process. If you feel highly focused and ready for maths, you may proceed to read. Ideally, finish the entire material first.

3.2 Getting more data on TradingView   
If your plan only allows for, for example, 20,000 bars on your chart at once you can still access up to the beginning of the OHLC data available for that specific instrument e.g., 200,000 bars, here is how.   
1\.   
Go to last available bar

2\.   
Click select the ’Last available bar’

3\.   
Click the beginning bar

4\.   
You get access to more bars

5\.   
Repeat.

Just because there is 3 months of data available on your platform does not mean you settle for it. You need more data.   
To know if a strategy is good you need up to years of data depending on the strategy’s timeframe. For example, the last 12 months+ for a day trading strategy (in-sample data) ideally more.   
1\.   
Additional evidence is required to support your strategy outside of main testing.

2\.   
You need out of sample data that supports your strategy to see if it works in favourable conditions under different macroeconomic situations. For example, strong intraday trends for trend following in different year(s).

3\.   
You need out of sample data that works heavily against your strategy to ’stress test’ your system for robustness. For example, periods of intense market trends for a mean reversion strategy. If your system collapses with drawdowns exceeding 30 risk units (equivalent to 30 losses) in adverse conditions. The strategy is not robust. Do not adjust the strategy to survive, if it does not survive, move on. Universal out of sample data rules and other nuances are covered later in other volumes.

4 Automation: Databento and Dukascopy   
For automated backtesting data, we recommend Databento (DB) and non-DMA market data vendors such as Dukascopy. If you cannot code but want to automate your testing, use a serious model such as Claude and manually review the code after model stress tests. To treat automated testing seriously, you must understand the language’s syntax, what the workflow actually does \+ the format of the datasets you are using, what information it provides and what it does not.   
If you choose to automate in the future, you cannot trust AI outputs blindly, and you will likely have to run tests locally when testing over moderate to large sample sizes.   
Understanding the granularity and accuracy of your data is just as important as your code’s integrity.   
While we see validity in automated testing, we cannot recommend it to beginners, as the potential for contaminants like overfitting and human error is too high without prior manual testing experience.   
4.1 Great backtesting tools are not free   
When money is scarce, many people will spend it on food but not on smarter investments. Many are defensive because a lot of services in the trading industry have deep conflicts of interest and unclear terms. Nobody likes guessing. I get that, I was in that position too. But it only delayed my progress. Regardless, saving time and hassle is something I learnt is worth paying for. I also realised that part of the resistance was ego. A lot of people who are not 100% sure a purchase will improve their efficiency avoid making the jump, even though it could turn out to be either a minor loss or a large gain.   
For example, you could have no bar replay and only a few months of data on your chart, settle for a low sample size (e.g., 50 trades), and fail live, or you can pay \<\$20 a month (or start a free trial) to backtest and get an acceptable sample (e.g., \>150 trades), giving   
yourself a higher chance of success when moving forward, as you have enough iterations to know the historical edge is real. It does not need to be TradingView; explore multiple backtesting platforms and pick the one that best suits your needs.   
5 Backtesting Costs Basics   
5.1 STS-Style Implied Backtesting Costs   
In this section, we talk about implied backtesting costs, with this protocol costs are set to at the extreme ends of what is realistic intraday.   
We apply:   
1\.   
Realistic but wide bid-ask spreads

2\.   
Pessimistic slippage

3\.   
Unfavourable swap charges

Calibration of costs:   
The costs are calibrated to poor but realistic unfavourable market conditions for that specific market on the venue the strategy is deployed on (broker, e.g., exchange or liquidity providers). For example, If a market has an average intraday spread of 2, the test will use 3 and an aggressive average slippage value, e.g., 0 to 2 ticks (minimum price movements) The way we calculate costs is designed to maintain consistent loss sizes, while applying predictable costs to realised profits.   
These values depend on:   
1\.   
The specific market,

2\.   
Your broker or exchange,

3\.   
Real data from minor research or experience,

4\.   
Your preferences (aggression),

5\.   
Your trading size (Market Influencing Sizes \= More Extreme).

Robustness under stress:   
The benefit of Implied Backtesting Costs is to review the strategy’s past return(s) for robustness. By requiring strategies to perform well under stressed cost assumptions, you reduce their sensitivity to a collapse in real time if trading conditions deteriorate.   
1\.   
The exchange due to poor liquidity provision on an exchange (thin order book)

2\.   
Your broker is less reliable than anticipated (FX and CFDs).

3\. You make mistakes (manual execution/human error) Implied backtesting costs factor these events into account indirectly.   
A real-time costs advantage:   
Realised costs are supposed to be lower than the costs we calculate (Implied), so that live trading benefits from a ‘cost advantage’ rather than being destroyed by a cost surprise. The ‘advantage’ itself is technically synthetic, but if a system only works when costs are applied as average or optimistic, it is rejected, as sustained periods of bad costs could destroy the strategy’s profitability.   
Acceptance criteria and protection of edge:   
We only accept strategies that remain profitable under our extreme cost assumptions. Money is on the line, and when market makers aren’t generous, we pay the price in cost discrepancies. By having this protocol in place, we erode the chance of abnormally high realised backtesting costs (real trading costs) from erasing the edge once the strategy is deployed.   
5.1.1 Base Calculations   
When you are in your strategy-building phase, our spreadsheets do this for you automat.ically. If you need to do this with different backtesting software or without assistance, here are some raw formulae and examples: Numbers required   
1\.   
Minimum stop size

2\.   
Spread value (higher end preferred)

3\.   
Slippage value (higher end preferred)

Example Values: ES Futures incl. \$6 commissions per contract (combined)   
5 minimum stop size $250percontract($5\*50) 0.25 minimum price movement, 0.5 bid-ask spread value, 0.125 avg slippage, commissions round trip value equal to 0.12 in price movement per contract (\$6)   
Calculating and combining your Implied Backtesting Costs (example)   
Stop loss minimum is \$5.00 and the impact of commissions is \$0.12 0.12÷5.00 \= 0.024 (2.4%) \-\> Commissions 0.5÷5.00 \= 0.1 (10%) \-\> Spread 0.125÷5.00 \= 0.025 (2.5%) \-\> Slippage   
Combined:   
The results, when applied using this method, will reflect a 12.4% reduction in profits and a 2.5% increase in losses under these unfavourable, though not unrealistic, conditions, as the spread is factored into limit order entries. These are your costs; a persistent pull on returns. For example, \-1R becomes \-1.025R in this case.   
For consistency, we apply this adjustment to profits so we can risk the same amount on each position, while still gaining at least 85.1% of the RRR on each profitable trade after costs. 2R would become 1.702R when the “max” costs are applied. Anomalous conditions can occur, but it is unlikely for costs to be this high because the market is highly liquid, spreads are often lower, and slippage is not usually this extreme. The wider the stop loss on an individual trade, the lower the relative costs are. If your exits are based on stops (trailing), you must also deduct slippage from profits, for example, 12.5% (base costs) \+ 2.5% (slippage). If you choose to use market orders, your on-paper slippage value must increase the cost of every entry.   
Why aggressive costs modelling is so important \-Ron (Verbatim from chats)   
Universal, regardless of the derivative.   
We always assume we get the worst cost value because when calculated based on historical data, for example, average stop sizes (higher than the minimum stop size), the cost profile   
can be optimistic, as it is extremely unlikely to repeat with 1:1 alignment with the tests (real-time costs are often worse than idealised).   
If the edge doesn’t persist in rough conditions, one bad spread and execution regime will   
finish you off.   
CFD Exclusive Insights “If gold’s bid-ask spread is 40 cents in the underlying market (GC Futures), but your   
platform shows a quote with a 20 cent bid-ask spread on gold CFDs (XAUUSD), the execution will often be poorer on most brokers, as this compensates for the discrepancy in live conditions. This is how liquidity providers tend to behave, but it is broker-dependent. This is often the result of last-look delays or liquidity provider directional risk manage.  
ment.” \-Direct extract from a discussion about someone’s trading system.   
How to set up positions to account for it properly in real time. (universal)   
Long (buy) positions: add the bid-ask spread size (what is quoted to you e.g., 0.25) to the price you want to be filled at, because your buy limit fills when the ask reaches your limit price, not the bid. ’I want to get filled at 10022’, the spread size is 0.25, 10022.25, buy limit.   
For short (sell) trades, add the spread to your stop loss, because you enter on the bid and exit as a buyer on the ask (which is beyond the bid). If your platform lets you switch the chart price from ask to bid (for example, on cTrader), you do not need to increase your stop size, because the stop will trigger off the correct price. Make sure your targets are also adjusted by the spread, as you are filled on the ask.   
If a trader wants to sell short at 10070 with a stop loss at 10080 and a target at 10050, and the bid-ask spread is 0.5, they would place a sell limit at 10070, a stop at 10080.5, and a target (buy limit) at 10050.5. Without these adjustments, if price misses the target by the spread, the position may not be closed, and the stop loss may be triggered prematurely if price misses it by the spread.   
These are the basics of how to get filled consistently where you intend, while keep.ing costs in check. This is one of many ways to manage costs with limit orders and must be changed to fit your strategy’s constraints. The primary benefit of the method shown is a higher chance that your position gets filled on efficient entries.   
Note 1:   
Some platforms shift the prices of your targets and stops if you receive slippage on entry. This is because they use a “price distance” method rather than a fixed price. To avoid this, make sure your target and stop values match what you initially placed, and add the spread to your stop (if short).   
5.2 Nuances Regarding Commissions   
For trading that includes commissions, we prefer to treat them as a fixed value embedded into costs. A fixed dollar cost per contract per round trip (completed trade) is common. By knowing what your minimum stop is, you know what percentage is deducted from your profits when it is factored into your trade per unit.   
Stocks:   
E.g., commissions that are equivalent to 1 cent per share in value when your stop loss is 20 cents is 5% in costs when isolated.   
(1 ÷ 20\) . 100 \= 5   
Futures:   
E.g., $6perEScontract(50units)commissions(includingexchangefees)whenyourstoplossminimumis10ticks($2.5x50 \= \$125 risk \$6 comms) units is 4.8% costs when isolated.   
(6 ÷ 125\) . 100 \= 4.8   
FX (USD quote):   
E.g., $7perEUR/USDcontract/lotcommissionswhenyourstoplossminimumis10pips($10 pip value per lot) comms are equal to 0.7 pips (\$7). 7% costs when isolated.   
\[10 . 10 \= 100\]   
(7 ÷ 100\) . 100 \= 7   
FX (Different quote currency vs account currency):   
E.g., $7perEUR/JPYcontract/lotcommissionswhenyourstoplossminimumis10pips($9.33 pip value per lot) comms are equal to 0.75 pips. 7.5% costs when isolated.   
P ipSize . LotSize Pip Value Per Lot \=   
Account Currency (USD) Quote Currency (JPY )   
To find the pip value we need the USD/JPY rate. In this example the rate will be USD/JPY Rate 107.20, 0.01 price movement \= 1 pip on USD/JPY   
P ipSize . LotSize \=0.01 . 100000 \= 1000   
1000   
Pip Value Per Lot \= ≈ \$9.33 EUR/JPY pip value per lot   
107.20   
The commissions are still \$7 per round trip but the pip value is 9.33 so now it is equal to   
0.75 pips for this pair. Calculation:   
7   
CommissionP ips \= ≈ 0.75 pips in net costs   
9.33 P ip V alue . MinimumSLsize \= Minimum Stop V alue P er Lot 9.33 . 10 \= 93.3

Commission%= . 100 ≈ 7.5% costs

Example figures only. Remember that pip sizes differ on a rate-by-rate basis, e.g., EUR/USD has a pip size of 0.0001.   
We suggest trading markets other than FX, as price discovery in FX tends to be more random, which can make it difficult to develop a genuine edge. These calculations are for educational purposes only.   
Exchange rates can be applied with similar principles for other asset classes, e.g., non-USA markets quoted in different currencies. Commissions, if any, must be included in the costs, not separately, so you can confirm that your costs are lower than 30%   
Futures example: $6incommissionsforESfuturespercontract(50.units)isequivalentto0.12inrawpricemovement($6 ÷ 50 \= \$0.12), so I would increase the backtesting costs via the slippage or spread value (for example, on our spreadsheets). For stocks, FX, or any other asset class, it would be the same procedure. If my spread value was 0.5 I factor all commissions into execution right away with a value of 0.62   
Nuance for percentage based costs   
If you have minimum stop sizes in percentages (Price distance) e.g., 0.1% minimum stop loss distance from entry. You would calculate commissions this way. 0.1% minimum stop with 0.01% comms \= 10% in costs. Example figures only. The highest costs from the highest rate are used. Trading costs are inevitable; they can only be reduced. Trading costs are not linear and have a persistent drag on returns.   
How costs are factored in and reduced must be changed on a strategy-to-strategy basis. A trader could have a 1:2 RRR 50% winrate with 20% costs (1.6R gains) and have a no-costs expectancy of 0.5R. Over 200 trades, the gain is not 100R net, it is 60R (a 40% reduction in returns rather than 20%) with 40% costs, there would be an 80% reduction in return (not including comms)   
(P rofitableOutcomes . AvgRatioBeforeCosts . Costs) . LosingOutcomes   
(100 . 2 . 80%) . 100 losses \= 60R Gain (40R Reduction from costs)   
(100 . 2 . 60%) . 100 losses \= 20R Gain (80R Reduction from costs)   
Poorly managed costs destroy gains (especially for scalpers, which is one of many reasons why we do not condone it). This is why you need a strong edge. The market demands efficiency (\>0.3R Expectancy minimum), and you need to factor in acceptable costs properly.   
5.3 Overnight Fees and Swing Trading Nuances   
Important: If you are trading futures, make sure you have enough overnight margin on your broker to hold overnight so the system is tradable. If you are trading FX or CFDs, make sure you calculate swap fees and account for them in your trading costs.   
If you do not intend to trade FX or CFDs, you may skip this section for now. Scroll to page 14\.   
5.3.1 A Simplified Walkthrough of Factoring Overnight Charges for Swing Trading Strategies.   
Do not be idealistic; put your strategy under stress. Count or get the holding time (from your test); for example, 2.36 days. We want to round this up to the nearest whole number for harshness, 2.36.3. We also assume we always pay swaps, never receive. Issue no mercy. One overnight hold when swaps are charged \= \+1 day.   
A simple method for calculating manually   
Net holding time for all positions in days number of positions   
(0+0+3+3+1)÷5 trades \= 1.4 days average holding time \-\> 2 days avg   
(0+0+3+3+1)/5 trades \= 1.4 days average holding time \-\> 2 days avg Financing costs (Dow Jones Example) \-6.5% rate per year (visit your platform’s symbol/instrument details to see).   
6.5   
\=0.0178%   
365   
A 0.0178% of your position value per night seems small until we do the maths. The highest US30/Dow Jones traded in the backtesting history was 48000 (example).   
0.0178% . 48000 \= 8.544pts   
This means that your position’s P\&L will decrease by a price equivalent of \> 8.5 points per daily close. If your minimum stop size is 50 points for this strategy, and the expected maximum spread for your session is 2 points and average slippage is expected to be 0.5 points (2.5pts total), your costs would be around 5% without swaps, but if the average holding time is 2 days, this trading cost jumps to over 30% for each position on average.   
8.544 . 2+2.5   
. 100 \= 39.17% Costs   
50   
This is too high \> 30%.   
Even without rounding up to 2 days. (1.4 days) your costs would be over 29.0% in costs which is far too high, so the trader is forced to do one of three things:   
1\.   
Increase the strategy’s stop-loss minimum to mitigate the adverse effect of swap fees and re-backtest to get the same values to recalculate.

2\.   
Cap holding time to one night and re-backtest to see if the profitability is still effective.

3\.   
Run the strategy in real time and get eaten alive by costs (Most traders do this).

Figure 2: Illustrating Section 3.3’s High Costs   
5.4 Why not close my position just before daily rollover instead of paying swaps?   
What happens   
If you do this on futures or CFDs, you will end up paying the price discrepancy across each daily close gap when you re-open your trade you will also face much wider bid-ask spreads (costs). This is often more expensive, and unpredictably so.   
Swap charges can be advantageous for swing trading.   
For example, a trader can hold overnight, accept gap risk, and pay a predictable fee. This makes swaps a fair payment, which makes leveraged overnight holding costs more consistent, therefore more calculable. If a trader closes their position at the end of the trading session (or they do not have enough maintenance margin to hold overnight), they will be forced to re-enter after the gap, where liquidity is poor. If the trader’s intention is to continue holding. In this situation, the trader has no way to know in advance whether price will gap against them, creating invisible costs. Swaps charges mitigate this issue by allowing you to hold overnight making them a fair exchange of value, making leveraged overnight holding both convenient and predictable as the initial position remains intact.   
Buy/Long example:   
That gap may be in your trade’s desired direction (forcing you to re-enter at a worse price) e.g., close 10000 open 10010 (with a wide spread/high cost to execute), or it may gap against your trade (giving you a small advantage) close 10000 open 9990 (discount).   
Although the probability price gaps up or down is roughly equal, the spread during the open is typically over four times the typical intraday amount (depending on the market) and the distribution of the net costs against the trader is unpredictable in the short term increasing randomness in results. In other words, you either keep your position for a predictable fee, or you face overnight gaps and poorer trading costs, paying a much larger bid-ask spread on each open. Futures are not a ticket out unless you are holding for week to months at a time. (Position trading)   
Nuance: The Ticket Out   
If you are holding indices such as the S\&P 500 over multiple days or weeks with low leverage (below 12x maximum) you will have enough maintenance margin to hold your position overnight without additional charges. It does not make sense to pay swaps via CFDs in this case. Note: A rollover on the next contract, which occurs once every 3 months, is the only time when you will need to re-adjust.   
This document’s primary function is to ensure you are aware of costs and the consequences that few take into account. If you feel confused, continue reading and revisit this PDF after you have finished reading the entire public material in your strategy development phase.   
5.5 Trading Success: How to Realistically Get It as a Smaller Trader?   
Do not enter the arena with just one strategy; You must have multiple logically tested systems that are uncorrelated to trade on separate accounts or prop firm evaluations. To increase your probability of success, you must spread but concentrate risk and take advantage of variance; do not take on excess vulnerability. Nobody knows exactly how the market will print tomorrow, next week, or the next month. High-risk percentages on lower funds allow for risk concentration, and aggressive compound steps allow for large growth opportunities with separate paths.   
Have at least three well-tested trading strategies in place before you start. Run each one separately, so that one strategy cannot adversely affect others.   
If you are struggling, we suggest you look into higher timeframes first (hourly or higher) or swing trade instead (hold over multiple days at a time).   
We ensure clarity at every step.   
Universal guidelines on how to perform STS-style capital partitioning are present within   
the public materials for regular conditions.   
Our mentees also receive simple, optimised withdrawal values based on stochastic calculus (Kolmogorov equations), which align with institutional risk management techniques such as “optimal stopping time” for hedge funds. We also have specialised methods for operating with retail prop firms.   
You have low intraday margins/high leverage. Use it to your advantage

Figure 3: 1% risk with 5k starting capital. Best of 100 simulated paths, 50 trades,   
1:2 RRR 50% Win rate Figure 4: 5% risk with 1k starting capital. Best of 100 simulated paths, 50 trades,

1:2 RRR 50% Win rate   
Trading is tough   
Nearly everyone fails.   
For professionals, the work is in the structure and review.   
If you do not know exactly why your edge should exist, you do not have one.   
You must force each strategy through a line of socratic questioning before deployment.   
1\.   
Clarification (“What exactly are the entry or exit rules here, in objective terms?” .Clarity),

2\.   
Assumption (“What market behaviour or conditions am I implicitly assuming for these rules to work?” \-Alignment),

3\.   
Evidence (“What backtest or live data do I have that shows this rule has positive expectancy?” \-Proof),

4\.   
Consequences (“What happens to drawdowns, my risk profile, and capital allocation if this rule is applied as designed?” \-Rigour),

5\.   
Alternative perspectives (“How would someone with the same knowledge as me view my strategy without context challenge it? \-Unbiased Frame”)

One good regime is all it takes to establish your trading career.   
That is what most people do not understand.   
The reason I love referring to my first profitable year in 2021 is because it taught me that making quadruple-digit percentage return(s) in a year does not take magic; all it takes is a favourable regime and fortitude, regardless of whether it is from trading or business.   
Many traders are led to believe it must take years and that they need to be “consistent”.   
However, without exclusive institutional tools which cost millions to license and maintain, a perpetual state of consistency, especially in day trading, often requires luck for long-term persistence on a strategy-by-strategy basis. Modern markets change too quickly for any single approach to work indefinitely. We call this inevitable breakdown ’edge decay’.   
Trading success itself does not depend on luck, it depends on taking safe, concentrated risks, superior exposure management, and withdrawals of accumulated profits.   
Trends do not last as long as they used to, and the market demands more from individuals to succeed. The market used to be about beating costs; the market now is about being first or the one who absorbs. Technically, it was always about being first, but priorities have shifted with more competitiveness.   
Reality:   
It is unrealistic to believe a day trading strategy will persist for long periods without changes, e.g., over ten years, or even 5 years in modern markets. That is extremely rare, and if seen in backtests with a decent sample, it is most likely overfitted and will not work in real time. Many testers fall into the perfect solution fallacy, which does not mix well with markets. No strategy maintains its prime effectiveness forever. The way we operate takes this into account.   
Closing Statements:   
Backtests do not collapse in real time because “markets are random”. Strategy fragility is a big problem for many traders.   
A robust system should still be effective if:   
• A Parameter change of ±20% does not collapse your edge.   
If a small parameter tweak ruins your system, it was curve-fit, not real. If you rely on data without stress tests and out-of-sample data, you might as well not test at all.   
Here are some examples:   
1\.   
If reducing or increasing your strategy’s target size by 20% makes your strategy unprofitable, that is not acceptable.

2\.   
Changing an indicator’s setting by ±20%, resulting in a strategy being half as effective, is not acceptable. Likely overfitted.

If your day trading or swing trading strategy does not degrade smoothly with slight adjustment, it is over-fitted.   
• Always ask yourself “What market mechanism would make this edge persist?”   
If you have to rely on narrative instead of facts, drop it. Backtests and forward tests are unlikely to forecast future results unless the trades are tied to a repeatable market behaviour (established microstructure effects, time-of.day behaviour, volatility expansions over sessions e.g., LDN/NY etc.). You need to ride the drift that the market offers you. There are many possibilities. Remember, noise does not repeat, but market mechanics do.   
• Variance:   
Most traders tend to discover variance the hard way, by assuming every losing streak means the system is broken and every winning streak means they understand markets. Neither of these interpretations survives contact with this chart. Do not fold.   
This basic guide is what I wish I had when I started.   
Before you start building, take your time to work through the rest our materials in the same steady way, one PDF at a time. You are laying the foundations now so the build feels cleaner, faster, and more confident.   
Thank you for reading.  
Ron \-Sentient Trading Society

16   
Mechanical vs Discretionary Trading: Clearing Up the Confusion   
Sentient Trading Society   
Strategy Engineering Volume 2

Ron & Ali   
Introduction   
There is the common idea that floats around endlessly; that discretionary intuitive trading means you’re being flexible and smart, while mechanical trading is some rigid, one-size-fits­all system that ignores the market context.   
That is an oversimplification.   
Mechanical systems can be flexible   
Think of mechanical systems like flowcharts or decision trees. They can include filters for volatility, time of day, higher timeframe context, session structure basically, anything you want to build in or as many nodes as you want if we’re imagining a flowchart/decision tree.   
You can even bake “discretion” into a mechanical system if you put in the work. Yes. Really.   
Traditional discretionary trading, by contrast, often feels smart because you’re calling the shots in real time with intuition. But if you don’t have clear rules backing your decisions, you’re prone to what I call   
Discretion as Reactive Price Making \-Ali   
You are far more susceptible to subconsciously or consciously registering and responding to recent stimuli (the last few trades), recent candles, sharp swings, or your overall performance. All of this is just noise. This is not a structured or a tested logical approach. By acting this way your trading reactions can exhibit recency bias \[1\]; how many traders reinforce this bias is through post-trade analysis journaling, and emotional trading could be masked as an exact, rigorous process.   
1   
This is Dangerous.   
If it is not testable logic; it is reactive bias masquerading as insight. Traders often do this with a post trade analysis journaling process.   
What you could be doing here is letting your natural pattern recognition (hu­man biology) override logic which leads to you overriding the process of trading with your instincts. You may think this is not the case, but you must realise that your pattern recognition will come first, and you will try to form some sort of logical reasoning as to why you saw such a pattern emerge on the chart.   
This forward-looking subjectivity on forward walks \[2\] leads to a lack of robustness and introduces a severe amount of fragility into your trading. \-Ali   
Every person’s brain chemistry is different, so the only person who should participate in a strategy that relies on intuition is the person deploying it. The system may ’work’ for that discretionary trader, but intuition-led success can only be replicated coincidentally. It is not objectively reproducible as nobody shares the same Central Nervous System and experience.   
Analogy:   
A discretionary trader adjusting to market noise actively or passively is like a Mechanical trader changing their system to produce better results in a back test (curve-fitting), but instead of overfitting a back test, it is your human biology (pattern recognition) pulling the strings on a walk forward. And that’s just as fragile for your system’s frame.   
Summary:   
Using intuition does not automatically make you smarter. Without clear, tested rules, it means more often than not for most traders that they’re trading messier.   
Traders occasionally find success relying on intuition, but they remain rare out­liers in a market that rewards systematic logic.   
Your system doesn’t have to be robotic or rigid. But your decision process needs to be accountable and repeatable. Otherwise, you’re apply­ing guesswork to some of the most efficient markets in the world.   
Recency Bias \[1\] \-Cognitive Bias when someone favours giving weight to recent behaviours whilst ignoring or downplaying longer term trends influencing trading behaviours. \-Basic example. A trader stops trading Wednesdays because the last 6 weeks have had losing Wednesdays but the strategy data over years has been net profitable on Wednesdays.   
Forward Walk/Walk Forward \[2\] \-Future price action and trading referring to real time trading or forward tests.   
Curve Fitting \[3\] \-When a strategy is tailored to fit past market data. When a system is tweaked to get better results on historical data.   
Why Confluence Can Hurt Your Trading   
Sentient Trading Society

Ron   
Understanding The Outcomes of Using Confluence   
Confluence is often seen as a way to bring more certainty to your trades, but it can actually introduce unnecessary noise and complexity. Relying on multiple factors to “confirm” a trade can create random elements in your system, leading to more variance and overfitting. It reduces your trading frequency, which might make your system look better in backtesting, giving false confidence in real time, but that is usually not the reality; Confluence should only be used when backed by tangible data, and never faith.   
For example, imagine your system would normally generate 100 trades in a backtest, but once you add confluence, the number of trades drops to 40\. With fewer trades, your results are more likely to look great, with higher win rates or profitability, but this is just an illusion. As you extend the backtest, the performance will likely decay, and you will see that it was not as effective as it first appeared.   
This is the confluence fallacy. Traders often think adding multiple layers of con­firmation improves their system, but all they are doing is overfitting it to certain conditions. The data looks promising in the short term, but it falls apart as you test further most of the time.   
Lots of traders get caught in the trap of confirmation bias or multiple time-frame top-down analysis. They rely on confluence from all kinds of sources, whether it is data-backed or intuitive; this just complicates or obfuscates the process and makes backtesting less reliable.   
In fact, educators often push this idea of using multi-timeframe analysis.   
This intentionally makes the strategy harder to backtest and leaves room for plausible deniability when the system fails, and so does using a system with discretionary influence.   
These added layers of complexity are pushed because they make it harder to expose ineffi­ciencies in their systems. When these educators sell you a strategy with multiple layers of confluence, it is not because it is the best way to trade. It is because the added complexity makes it difficult to see that the system will ultimately average out and fail over time, or to add confidence to the user. This creates a veil around the strategy, hiding its weaknesses, and making it look like there is something valuable there when there really is not.   
This is why simple indicators systems and so on, get ridiculed as the veil is easily removed.   
1   
Ideally when you are on the right side of the market, all the confirmation you need should already be in the price action. That is why I use limit orders to execute. I do not need extra confluence. I just need a solid system and the discipline to stick to it.   
Especially when shorting, if you are waiting for extra confirmation signals, you are already behind the market. It moves quickly, and the more confirmation you need, the more likely you are to miss opportunities.   
So do not get fooled. The idea that more confluence equals better trading is a trap. Keep it simple, trust your system, and avoid overcomplicating things just because the “experts” tell you to.   
2 Summary   
Do not use confluence or multiple timeframes just for the sake of it.   
Be vigilant when analysing the intent behind what you are shown over data.   
3 Additional Reading   
Data Snooping (significant)

[https\://quant.fish/wiki/data-snooping-in-algorithmic-trading/](https://quant.fish/wiki/data-snooping-in-algorithmic-trading/)

Multi Timeframe Analysis: 3 Trader Examples   
Sentient Trading Society

Ron   
Multi Timeframe (MTF) Analysis: 3 Trader Examples   
Traders must stick to a single setup on a single timeframe in the beginning. Multiple time-frames are often an unnecessary complication and easily result in data snooping \[5\]. See MTFs as a potential optimisation to optimise a strategy, it is not an essential complication.   
Using multiple timeframes is okay if it is used in a consistent and controlled way \[1\]. Example: a trader could consistently look at three timeframes, always top down or always bottom up, but consistent \[1\].   
1 Trader A: Day-Swing Hybrid (Higher to Lower)   
Trader A uses daily charts, hourly charts, and executes on the 5 minute chart. There are absolutely no deviations. There is nothing open to interpretation and nothing intuitive. There are no thoughts such as “sometimes I start on this timeframe first, sometimes this one.” If there is variance where he starts, it must be because of a predefined, logical, tested rule.   
For example, Trader A, who day-swing trades S\&P 500, might only short on the hourly timeframe on ES because S\&P 500 is unlikely to trend downward for longer periods of time. He does this to reduce his holding times. Factual and logical. Acceptable. That is why he mixes timeframes \[1\].   
2 Trader B: Day Trader (Lower to Higher)   
He uses the 5m, 15m, hourly, and daily. Just like Trader A, he uses multiple timeframes on a tested system with no deviations. Nothing open to interpretation and no discretion.   
Trader B looks for setups on the 5m and 15m charts. When his setup presents itself, he enters. Trader B trades reversals exclusively and has rules for it. Trader B looks on the 5m and 15m charts for entries. Whichever presents itself, if he gets a setup on either timeframe, he will execute the next on the other timeframe too. For example, he gets a buy on 5m first. He enters with 2% risk, and for 15m he enters 2% risk on that trade too (he can frame it however he likes as long as tested), these are for example purposes only.   
1   
Trader B uses the hourly timeframe for manually trailing his stop loss behind lows (he has a specific ruleset for it). Trader B uses the daily timeframe to set hard targets for his positions. He runs the winner on the hourly and has a hard target using the daily. That is why he mixes timeframes. This sounds fine \[1\].   
3 Trader C: The Disorganised (Like Most MTF Analysis Traders)   
He trades most timeframes, from 5m to weekly. Trader C trades Forex USD pairs. He looks at a large set of data aimlessly. Sometimes he will start at the Dollar Index (DXY) to measure things like “strength”, or the pair he is trading, usually high timeframes such as daily or weekly to get “bias”, COT reports, or any data needed to confirm his biases.   
He tries to establish a “narrative” or draw an “institutional story” through usu­ally vigorous, although sometimes meticulous, data snooping \[5\] (repeatedly look­ing at the same dataset to get a desired outcome), for example, looking at higher timeframe marking points or connecting structure inconsistently.   
He can justify why he is doing X and Y in real time using words. Let us give him the benefit of the doubt here. Maybe he is even backing it up with logical principles like order flow mechanics. He has a hard time putting it into fixed rules.   
There are many factors that can influence his trading, some not accounted for in testing. Trader C usually does not backtest properly. If he tried, it would be hard to be unbiased or accurate. It is easy to get a false sense of security in the system since future data visibility can influence backtesting systems of this nature (even if subconsciously). It is very dangerous.   
Conveniently, this flaw makes his system harder to unveil as ineffective as well (it usually is).   
Trader C could consistently start on, for example, the weekly, but does not consistently mark the chart. He is looking for something, but it is not linear.   
For example, Trader C could use fixed, predefined rules to look for something specific on the daily or weekly (X), look for something specific again on the hourly to support what was identified on the higher timeframe (Y), and execute on the 5m using a specific entry method \[Z\]. The problem is, for Trader C, (X) and   
(Y) are usually inconsistent, sometimes (Z) too, or he is looking for many setups instead of a single setup, which introduces noise to the trading method (results can depend on what comes first). For example, a retail trader could trade based on Entry technique 1, 2, and 3 lined up on multiple timeframes, but if he has seen technique 2 and 3 first, he could act on technique 4 instead of 3\. Or he could enter technique 3, get stopped out, and miss technique 4 if he is rigid either way. This has many logical fallacies.   
I want you to feel the chaos, the lack of order.   
When I see traders operate like this, I think to myself, has he forgotten he is analysing a dataset or trying to sell the market an idea or pitch a sale. They look lost in the noise.   
Naming All The Biases Trader C Suffers From   
Also remember that not every Trader C is exactly the same.   
•   
Confirmation bias

•   
Appeal to complexity (convoluted setup)

•   
Post hoc ergo propter hoc (assuming that just because one technique occurred before another, that it was the cause of that event. For example, technique 2 happening before technique 4 does not mean that technique 2 caused technique 4.)

•   
False dilemma (possibly if there is favouritism between technique 3 over 4, and not considering co-existence)

•   
Inconsistency fallacy

•   
Cherry picking

•   
Fallacy of composition

•   
Appeal to complexity (if the system is complex in an overt manner). Trader C could think, “the market is complicated so my system must be too.”

•   
Ad hoc reasoning (if Trader C constantly adjusts rules or explanations to justify results, often seen in traders who journal their trades too)

•   
Subjectivist fallacy (if intuition is used, a setup could be justified because the trader believes in it, which works for me logically)

•   
Circular reasoning (if he uses many sources to line up his bias for execution, he may believe something is valid because he acted on it)

•   
Cognitive dissonance (for example, needing confluence but entering on discretionary setups)

•   
Narrative fallacy (for example, “this is a liquidity sweep before a big move”) to rationalise a trade that lacks his actual setup alignment

dredging
